  > 
  > Young said he’d be surprised if local news is still a focus for national philanthropy in eight years. “Filling your cup up with money that will be impossible to recover in four years is a danger zone,” he added.
  > 
  > Right now, more than 80% of Planet Detroit’s revenue mix comes from foundations. Ignaczak said the organization hopes to bring that down to 50% by 2028. “But for nonprofit newsrooms covering environmental and public health issues in a mid-size metro, general operating grants aren’t a crutch,” she said. “They’re infrastructure.”
  > 
  > As for Press Forward, “Time will tell, as these grants go away, what impact [they’ve] actually had on the newsrooms that received [them], how many have managed to build sustainability,” she added. “I think a newsroom that needed $50,000 to survive to next year had problems that $50,000 isn’t going to solve.”
  > 
  > LOOKOUT executive director Jake Hylton believes there’s enough funding to go around. “While there may not be enough to support every single newsroom at a very large amount,” he said, “I do believe that there is a larger investment in news that could be done.”
  > 
  > A second grantee who asked to stay anonymous was blunt about their fears for the future. “We’re scared shitless about 2027,” this person said, “because not only is the Press Forward money not returning, we also see none of the national grants that sustained us in 2024 and previous years.”
  > 
  > “We’d love to be thriving, but we’re happy to continue to survive”
  > The Copper River Record was in the black before receiving this grant, and the lean paper’s fixed overhead remains low. For the Record, Press Forward has been like “a fairy godmother,” Sayer said.
  > 
  > “I’m not going to lie: I will miss having the money to say yes to any reasonable story,” she continued. “But I think that, even going back to our original budget, the strength of what we learned will continue to help us deliver a higher quality paper that is more relevant to the communities we serve.”
  > 
  > The Sandpoint Reader, Olson said, is “surviving” right now. “We’d love to be thriving,” he added, “but we’re happy to continue to survive when many print publications aren’t.” He credited the Press Forward grant with giving the burned-out team “the chance to fall in love with our industry again.”
  > 
  > Sporleder Orr feels nervous about what’s ahead, but with the World Journal in a much more comfortable cash flow position, she and her husband are “in a way better mental state about the business.”
  > 
  > “We feel good about the future right now, that we are going to be able to keep going and make it,” she said. “We’re back to, ‘This is what we do ’til we die.'”
  > 
  > Grantees are included in LION’s public database capturing median staffing, revenue, and audience numbers from audits completed by more than 400 newsrooms. LION’s data suggests local newsrooms with budgets under $1 million are common; about three-quarters of the newsrooms that completed its most recent audit reported revenue under $1 million.
  > Press Forward paused grants to the St. Louis Argus after other local outlets reported the organization was taking their work without permission. Press Forward required the Argus to complete training from the Maynard Institute, then gave it the funds.
  > Not all the successful hires have journalism backgrounds. One current managing editor is a former school superintendent. “Every day that we have to teach someone from another field how to craft a lede, or cover a public meeting, or sell an ad by column inches, we are still grateful,” Brannon said.
  > “For this program, the timeline was tight,” Anglin said. “Our grantees have told us where we can do better, including offering coaching sooner, and we plan to incorporate that feedback in the future.” The open call launched a month after Anglin was hired, which meant she was still hiring the rest of Press Forward’s staff. Press Forward did not hire Graham Ringo, the grantee engagement and support manager who built out the program pairing audits and coaching, until several months later.

### 5. Only 32% of Americans have high confidence that news orgs will report midterm election results accurately, survey finds
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：[原文链接](<https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/only-32-of-americans-have-high-confidence-that-news-orgs-will-report-midterm-election-results-accurately/>)
- 发布时间：Thu, 29 Oct 2026 18:35:18 +0000
- 全文：
  > About six in 10 Americans said they have at least “some” confidence that news organizations will report the upcoming midterm election results accurately, according to a new national survey. But only 32% said they had “quite a bit” or “a great deal” of confidence that journalists would do the job.
  > 
  > In the survey from Medill Local News Initiative and the Alliance for Trust in Media, 37% said they had either “not too much confidence” or “no confidence at all” that news outlets would accurately report the election results.
  > 
  > The survey was completed by a nationally representative sample of 1,057 U.S. adults either online or by telephone earlier this month. The findings echo other national surveys, including a Gallup survey that showed trust in media to report “fully, accurately, and fairly” at 33% — an increase from an all-time low last year.
  > 
  > The Medill/Alliance survey found about half of Americans said they had “not too much confidence” or “no confidence at all” in news outlets to investigate voter fraud claims thoroughly. U.S. President Donald Trump has cast doubt on elections on 70% of days since returning to office — starting with false claims about voter fraud on the first day of his second term.
  > 
  > In another bleak result from the same poll, slightly more survey respondents said journalists manipulate the news for political or commercial purposes than report it accurately. Across the survey, Democrats reported being more confident in (and trusting of) news organizations than Republicans. Nearly 80% of Republicans said journalists try to manipulate the news compared to 38% of independents and 24% of Democrats.
  > 
  > More on the survey, conducted by NORC at the University of Chicago, here.

### 6. The New York Times is adding more AI features to its Cooking app, and users aren’t thrilled
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：[原文链接](<https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/the-new-york-times-is-adding-more-ai-features-to-its-cooking-app-and-users-arent-thrilled/>)
- 发布时间：Wed, 28 Oct 2026 18:51:20 +0000
- 全文：
  > Sometimes, adding just one new ingredient to a stew can ruin the whole thing.[sidenote]Olives, yecch.[/sidenote] And that’s how at least some New York Times readers see its addition of AI features to its popular Cooking app.
  > 
  > Here’s Genevieve Ko, NYT Cooking senior editor and columnist, explaining the new features:
  > 
  > I naïvely assumed A.I. wouldn’t affect my work as a recipe developer and my role at NYT Cooking, where I oversee the editorial side of our recipes and related stories. But as artificial intelligence improved, our product and engineering colleagues saw its potential to help us create features Cooking users wanted.
  > 
  > We’ve just launched our latest tool, which gives cooks the ability to apply modifications to our recipes. We’re rolling it out gradually to monitor how it performs (whether it’s working fast enough, for instance) and to see how people are using it (as of now, we’re getting a lot of requests for ingredient substitutions). The tool is currently available to all Cooking subscribers on our iOS app, and we plan to make it available more broadly soon.
  > 
  > The Times had previously rolled out AI features aimed at converting volume-based and weight-based measurements (“What’s one cup of bok choy in grams?”) and scaling a recipe up or down to serve more or fewer people. The main new feature is focused on ingredient substitutions.
  > 
  > Say you’re cooking braised white beans and greens with parmesan, NYT Cooking app in hand. But you notice that you’re out of chard and only have spinach. Cooking veterans know that chard is a rough-and-tumble green that has some backbone[sidenote]There’s a reason one type of chard is known as “perpetual spinach.”[/sidenote], while spinach is a comparative wimp that will wilt the moment you look at it funny. If you need to sub in spinach for chard, how would the recipe change? Would you, for instance, add the spinach later than you would the chard to account for its pathetic stamina? That’s the sort of thing the new AI feature could account for.
  > 
  > Cooking staffers “spent countless hours evaluating sample after sample to get the chatbot answers to closely align with how we would answer as culinary experts,” Ko writes. “We applied the same rigor as we do to editing, grounded in Times standards.”
  > 
  > But because Ko knows that any AI-powered features are likely to be controversial with a Times audience, she spends quite a lot of space addressing potential objections, everything from “the impact of data centers” to “the concentration of power among A.I. companies” and “the risk of A.I. escaping our control.” These features are designed to enable “the tactile physical pleasure of cooking” and getting a “very real, very human creation” on your table.
  > 
  > Broadly speaking, commenters weren’t buying it.
  > 
  > “What a massive disappointment,” wrote Susan from New Jersey. “The internet is full of AI cooking content. I pay you because I prefer mine done by people.”
  > 
  > “This decision misses the mark by such a wide margin it’s baffling,” says Ian from Los Angeles. “And the disclosure just as baffling. It would not be inaccurate to summarize the piece as saying ‘I know doing this is bad and I don’t want to be associated with the bad thing so that’s why we are doing the bad thing.'”
  > 
  > “Nobody wants this, it’s bad for cooking, it’s bad for the NYT,” writes Bill from Pittsburgh. “Wasting a column trying to gaslight yourself and your audience into believing that it’s anything but is an embarrassment and a waste of everyone’s time.”
  > 
  > “PLEASE PLEASE PLEASE DO NOT DO THIS,” says Judah Mann of New York. “Oh, wait, you already did. Ok, I’m out.”
  > 
  > Or as Naomi from California puts it: “I am sad that AI has breached the one online resource I truly love.”
  > 
  > Because I enjoy the irony, I scraped the 171 comments left on the Times’ announcement and asked Codex (running GPT-6.1 Sol, at medium thinking) to do some sentiment analysis. (Twelve of the 171 were comments left by Times staffers; I’m excluding them here.) Roughly 77% of the reader comments were negative about the new AI features, it found, versus 14% positive and the remainder neutral or mixed. Among the biggest categories of complaint: This shouldn’t require AI. AI is destroying the environment. AI is not why I’m paying the Times $390 a year. The adjustments the AI produces are going to be bad.
  > 
  > Dozens of commenters asked for a way to turn off the feature. “No, not at this time,” Ko responded, “though this feature is completely optional to use.” Indeed: Don’t ask AI to substitute your chard and AI won’t substitute your chard.
  > 
  > One consistent thread was that cooking is supposed to present you with difficulties and challenges and the whole point is figuring them out by yourself. “Anyone using AI for cooking has no sense of taste and no desire to work out the intricacies of cooking and baking,” writes Michael King from Brooklyn. “To use AI for recipes is beyond the pale. If you are going to use AI, then just cut to the chase and defenestrate yourself.” Or BJ from North Carolina: “So, you’re telling me that I’m too stupid to figure it out on my own?”
  > 
  > Meanwhile, the few positive comments tended to focus on how the AI feature had, in fact, made dietary substitutions easier for them. “I love the new AI feature because it quickly (and so far) accurately converts recipes to gluten free,” Anne Wagner from Kansas City writes. “I don’t cook for stress relief,” says Andy from San Francisco. “I cook to put dinner on the table. Anything that makes the process a little faster or smoother is welcome.”

### 7. Meta pledges to consult publishers before finalizing its new terms
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：[原文链接](<https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/meta-pledges-to-consult-publishers-before-finalizing-its-new-terms/>)
- 发布时间：Wed, 28 Oct 2026 18:23:04 +0000
- 全文：
  > Meta will complete “a full consultation” with news industry leaders before implementing its controversial new terms — even if it means another delay, an executive for the social media company announced today.
  > 
  > “We’ve heard publishers’ feedback on our proposed ads and business terms update. We care deeply about our partners and do not plan to make policy changes without a full consultation with industry groups and leaders,” vice president of communications Andy Stone tweeted. “We’re committed to getting this right, and as we expect this consultation could extend beyond November 20, in which case we’d delay implementation of the updated terms. Until then our existing terms remain in place.”
  > 
  > Meta did not answer Nieman Lab’s questions about which news industry groups and leaders Meta plans to consult.
  > 
  > The proposed terms have drawn criticism because they would permit broad scraping of websites “associated with” business accounts, and potentially allow Meta to use that scraped content to train its AI models, with no option to opt out. Last week, Meta announced it would push back implementation of its new terms to November 20 in order to “explain to our partners and stakeholders what’s changing and what’s not — including how Meta uses web crawlers and the fact that content owners will still be able to use robots.txt to signal preferences,” a Meta spokesperson told Nieman Lab. Meta had previously revised its new terms to remove the word “train.” No version of the terms has included language about respecting robots.txt preferences.
  > 
  > Shortly after Stone’s announcement, a Norwegian court blocked Meta’s terms from taking effect on November 20 until the court had assessed their legality, the country’s public broadcaster NRK reported.
  > 
  > Meta’s controversial terms
  > October 15Meta’s new commercial terms will allow the scraping of news sites, legal experts say
  > October 17Legal experts warn Meta’s new terms still grant broad scraping permissions, despite language changes
  > October 21U.K. politicians and German publishers call on regulators to pause Meta’s new terms before October 30 deadline
  > October 22Publishers pressure Meta to nix new terms that would let the company scrape their websites
  > October 26Meta delays rollout of terms that would allow broad scraping of news sites. Legal experts are still skeptical.
  > October 27“A gross abuse of Meta’s power”: California senator Adam Schiff tells Meta to scrap its new terms of service
  > October 28 ★Meta pledges to consult publishers before finalizing its new terms
  > 
  > The ruling comes one day after the Norwegian Media Businesses’ Association (MBL) filed the first known lawsuit filed against Meta over its commercial terms. MBL represents a coalition of major Norwegian news publishers, including the country’s largest newspaper groups and NRK.
  > 
  > “We are satisfied that the court has agreed with us that Meta holds a dominant position in the relevant market and that the new terms are unreasonable,” said Lene Eltvik Vindfeld, MBL’s board chair, in a news release. “We are also pleased that the court recognizes that the alternative is that media companies risk losing control over editorial content built up over many years.”
  > 
  > Since Nieman Lab first reported on the terms on October 15, publishers and politicians around the world have pushed back, filing complaints with national competition authorities, calling on the European Commission to investigate, and writing letters to Meta’s CEO Mark Zuckerberg. Two United States senators — California Democrat Adam Schiff and Tennessee Republican Marsha Blackburn — each separately wrote to Zuckerberg, insisting that the company withdraw the terms as they’re currently written.
  > 
  > “After I raised serious concerns about Meta’s plans to scrape and abuse creators’ content, I am pleased the company is finally acknowledging the concerns of publishers and content creators,” Blackburn said in a statement to Nieman Lab on Wednesday. “While this is a step in the right direction, a vague promise to consult with industry stakeholders and potentially delay implementation is not nearly enough. Meta must commit to meaningful changes that protect the intellectual property rights of American creators, and I will continue holding the company accountable until it does.”
  > 
  > On October 21, the European Publishers Council signed onto a joint letter to the European Commission, asking the Commission to block the terms from taking effect. The letter claims that Meta’s terms violate the Digital Markets Act, a law passed in 2020 to curb the dominance of big tech companies and create fairer competition across the region.
  > 
  > EPC executive director Angela Mills Wade said on Wednesday that the organization has not heard from Meta about its letter or its concerns.
  > 
  > “We welcome Meta’s offer to consult publishers fully, and to delay implementation if necessary,” Mills Wade said in an email to Nieman Lab. “However, that consultation must lead to binding contractual safeguards…Using Meta’s services to reach audiences or advertise should not mean granting wider rights to exploit publishers’ journalism, whether collected from websites or uploaded to its platforms. Publishers must retain control over additional uses of their content, including for AI, and the ability to negotiate licenses and payment.”
  > 
  > This story has been updated to include a statement from Marsha Blackburn.

### 8. Vox launches its first app with an eye toward building paid memberships
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：[原文链接](<https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/vox-launches-its-first-app-with-an-eye-toward-building-paid-memberships/>)
- 发布时间：Wed, 28 Oct 2026 12:02:57 +0000
- 全文：
  > Vox has launched its first mobile application, the publication best known for its explanatory journalism announced Wednesday morning.
  > 
  > The app, which puts Vox’s text, audio, and video in one feed, is the company’s latest bet on growing paid membership. Users can choose between a home tab of featured pieces or a chronological feed of the latest stories. While the app is free to download and browse, it’s catered toward serving Vox journalism to paid subscribers in one place. Vertical videos and podcasts are free to watch and listen to, but only paid subscribers can read text stories and listen to podcasts ad-free.
  > 
  > Vox declined to share specific paid membership numbers, though the company said in May that it had “tens of thousands of paid subscribers.” The program was launched in May 2024. James Murdoch — son of media mogul Rupert Murdoch — purchased Vox.com along with the Vox Media Podcast Network and New York Magazine for more than $300 million in May 2026.
  > 
  > Growing membership is a “key priority,” managing editor for audience and membership Bill Carey said. Vox believes launching the app is one way to do that. A Vox membership costs $5 per month, or $60 per year, and offers ad-free versions of podcasts and unlimited access to all of the website’s contents.
  > 
  > Strengthening its relationship to its audience is only becoming more important for Vox. The app launch comes at a time when news publishers are increasingly trying to keep users on their own platforms amid growing preferences for AI summaries, news creators, and industry-wide traffic declines.
  > 
  > “The move toward developing our membership program in the last couple of years has really been about understanding how important it is for us to build more community and to foster that loyalty [of our] audience,” Carey said. “When we were looking at that, it really felt like an app was a good way to continue pointing in that direction.”
  > 
  > Vox isn’t the only news outlet focusing on mobile apps. The Verge — Vox’s former sister publication under Vox Media — named traffic declines as one of the reasons why it plans to launch its own app next year. The Wall Street Journal’s head of digital, Taneth Evans, recently said that the Journal makes its app more useful to users by offering all of its mediums in one place. Evans also said that news publishers “need to give people reasons to open an app beyond simply offering news.”
  > 
  > “Websites will become a service for agents,” Evans said. “Apps are gonna be the thing that humans will connect with.”
  > 
  > Vox declined to provide website traffic numbers, but data from Comscore shows that the website’s unique visitors decreased by 36% year-over-year between September 2025 (7.4 million) and September 2026 (4.8 million). Archived data from SimilarWeb shows the site had 20.8 million visits in September 2021.
  > 
  > Vox is one of the most popular news brands on YouTube with more than 12.7 million followers. The publication has one million Instagram followers, plus Substack subscribers and paid Patreon members. Vox launched its Patreon in November 2025, though its two video series exclusive to the platform have not been updated since January.
  > 
  > “Even if there wasn’t a decline in search traffic, as everyone has been seeing — and we still get members from search — we want to create that relationship with our audience,” publisher and editor-in-chief Swati Sharma said. “People know Vox and they have a relationship with Vox. We have a younger audience that’s on all of these different platforms. I think it is definitely a part of building that relationship ourselves.”
  > 
  > That the app makes all Vox content accessible in one place for current and future members also signals the “un-siloing” of the newsroom overall, Sharma said. Vox currently has 78 editorial employees.
  > 
  > When Sharma, who has helmed Vox.com since 2021, first arrived, she found that Vox’s main mediums (text, audio, and video) “weren’t in service of one another.” The “silos” allowed teams to build their own audiences and products. But while teams discussed story ideas with each other and during editorial meetings, “they all [needed] to go into their creative spaces and find what works for their medium and not be in service of just one larger section.”
  > 
  > That’s changed over the past five years, Sharma said. Each team — text, video, and audio — does a little bit of everything now. Text reporters appear on podcasts and in videos, while podcasters and hosts also write their own newsletters.
  > 
  > “In a lot of newsrooms, there’s one dominant form, and at Vox, there wasn’t. They’re all really important,” Sharma said. “[Senior correspondent] Dylan Scott is on our Unexplainable podcast talking about Alzheimer’s. [Host and editorial director] Astead Herndon does a newsletter and a Substack Live every week, and he does a podcast. That’s the future for us, and that’s where our whole newsroom is moving, where those silos are done and you can basically do everything.”

### 9. Creators who show their sources build trust, survey finds
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：[原文链接](<https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/creators-who-show-their-sources-build-trust-survey-finds/>)
- 发布时间：Tue, 27 Oct 2026 15:00:48 +0000
- 全文：
  > About one in five U.S. adults regularly gets their news from news creators on social media platforms, but it can be difficult for audiences to suss out which personalities to trust amid an avalanche of misinformation, hot takes, and polarizing content.
  > 
  > New research spearheaded by a team of journalists and political scientists shows that giving audiences a peek into the reporting process can help bolster their trust in the news.
  > 
  > Proof News, a nonprofit newsroom focused on tech accountability, in collaboration with researchers at the French university Sciences Po, shared social news videos with 2,000 U.S. adults to measure their perceptions of trustworthiness. Participants were randomly assigned one of Proof’s yet-to-be-published short-form news videos.
  > 
  > Some participants viewed videos with labels describing the sources, others saw videos with labels describing the newsroom’s ethical standards, and some saw videos without a label. The Sources labels appeared on cards embedded at the end of the video, like a credits reel, or were placed into the video description, the text accompanying the video.
  > 
  > [img wide]When viewing a short-form news video, survey respondents saw a label noting sources, newsroom standards, or no label at all.[/img]
  > 
  > Participants then rated the videos on various trust metrics. We found that labeling videos did increase trust — particularly a label that described the video sources in the description and, to a slightly lesser extent, a label describing newsroom standards in the credits.
  > 
  > This effect was particularly prominent among survey respondents who identified as Democrats or reported having high trust in science. Those groups, when shown the sourcing label in the description of a general news video, reported higher assessments of transparency and trust in the journalist.
  > 
  > There were fewer statistically significant increases in trust metrics with labels for text articles, but Sally Lehrman, founder of The Trust Project, said the findings were still intriguing.
  > 
  > “I’m excited to see the results,” Lehrman said. “Methods and references really do resonate…People are asking, where did the journalist get this information, and how can I trust it.”
  > 
  > [img wide][/img]
  > 
  > Lack of standards
  > As audiences increasingly turn to social media for news, there is a growing public discussion about how trustworthy creators are, especially when it comes to disclosing their funding and relationships. In January, The Wall Street Journal wrote about corporate and foreign interests using influencers to push agendas. In August, The Washington Post reported that the Pentagon secretly hired conservative military veterans with large online audiences to amplify Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth’s messaging, and a New York Times investigation revealed online influencers received funding from political campaigns without disclosing the connection in their videos.
  > 
  > Some creators have begun trying to distinguish themselves from this type of behavior through their own disclosure efforts.
  > 
  > Journalist and creator Dave Jorgenson, who has nearly 1 million followers across TikTok, YouTube, and Instagram, has started publishing a transparency report on his website, Local News International, revealing his sources of funding alongside his editorial standards.
  > 
  > Ultimately, he thinks disclosures of political funding should no longer be voluntary but mandatory. “Platforms should be regulating themselves,” Jorgenson said in an interview. “You can’t really force that unless there are laws.”
  > 
  > Proof used Jorgenson’s ethics and transparency statement as a model for the newsroom standards label in video testing and found that it increased viewers’ trust in the news outlet and the likelihood that they would share a general news story.
  > 
  > New York City is already considering disclosure requirements in the wake of revelations in the Columbia Journalism Review that exposed the inner workings of New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani’s connections with influencers. The author of the article, Anya Schiffrin, a senior lecturer at Columbia University’s School of International and Public Affairs, testified at a September city council meeting that creator relationships — both paid and unpaid — should be disclosed to viewers.
  > 
  > “It’s important for democracy that we have standards,” Schiffrin said.
  > 
  > Proof for a new model
  > Proof News, which launched in 2024, pioneered an Ingredients label that takes cues from the scientific method and names the journalists’ hypothesis, sources, limitations, and the story’s findings.
  > 
  > To test whether the label worked to increase viewer trust for short-form vertical videos, we stripped down the Ingredients label to a bullet list of sources followed by an asterisk denoting the reporting limitations. The survey showed the label had a consistent positive effect when placed in the video description, the text box that is often located under the video.
  > 
  > The team also tested how labels performed on online text news articles. The survey showed 1,600 U.S. participants original, unpublished Proof News stories without a label, with a Proof Ingredients label, a label modeled after the Trust Project that described the reporting process, and a label describing newsroom standards.
  > 
  > [img wide]Journalists designed three different labels: one about the newsroom’s standards, one about the reporting process, and one inspired by the scientific method, stating a hypothesis, sources, limitations, and findings.[/img]
  > 
  > There were fewer statistically significant increases in trust metrics with the print labels. The most significant findings were an increase in perception of transparency when the reporting process label was used on a polarizing story and an investigative story.
  > 
  > One possible explanation for the differing results between labeling video and text articles is the “ergonomics” of the medium, said Damien Bol, a professor at the Center for Political Research at Sciences Po who helped lead the survey design and data analysis.
  > 
  > “When reading a text, for example, people can quickly skim through it or skip parts of it, whereas a video forces them to follow the story second by second, even when watching at double speed,” Bol said. Different formats, Bol said, shape the “experience of consuming a news story.”
  > 
  > Influencing the influential
  > Earlier this month, Julia Angwin, an author of this article, hosted a private convening of creators at Harvard Kennedy School’s Shorenstein Center on Media, Politics and Public Policy to discuss creating shared standards that could be conveyed through a label or other public statement.
  > 
  > The group of 30 leading content creators from news, science communication, education, history, and other evidence-based fields discussed how they could distinguish their work by signaling to viewers their commitment to standards that could include disclosing funding sources and the use of artificial intelligence. Creator-led efforts to bring this to fruition are ongoing.
  > 
  > San Diego-based journalist Lisa Remillard, who has more than 4 million followers on her TikTok channel “The News Girl,” said that making videos with verified facts can feel like a small club. But if she can’t independently confirm something, she’s “not putting that out there.”
  > 
  > Remillard’s audience appears to appreciate transparency. Earlier this year, she published a video describing her editorial standards — and was richly rewarded for it. “I think clearly defining those ethics and standards resonated with my audience and gave them a reason to want to financially support my work,” Remillard said.
  > 
  > Jessica Yellin, the founder of the political podcast, YouTube channel, and social media brand “News Not Noise,” has also called for news creators to voluntarily sign a Creator Constitution, agreeing to journalistic standards such as citing sources, fixing errors, and disclosing financial relationships.
  > 
  > She said labeling sources is about the “signal.” Even if audiences don’t take the time to digest every word, Yellin observed, “It’s the act of showing it that matters.”
  > 
  > Julia Angwin is the founder of Proof News and the founding director of the Independent Media + Audience Project at the Shorenstein Center on Media, Politics and Public Policy at Harvard Kennedy School. Annie Gilbertson is managing editor of Proof News. Rina Palta is editor-at-large at Proof News and senior data scientist at the Independent Media + Audience Project.

### 10. “A gross abuse of Meta’s power”: California senator Adam Schiff tells Meta to scrap its new terms of service
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：[原文链接](<https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/a-gross-abuse-of-metas-power-california-senator-adam-schiff-tells-meta-to-scrap-its-new-terms-of-service/>)
- 发布时间：Tue, 27 Oct 2026 13:52:44 +0000
- 全文：
  > Democratic Senator Adam Schiff of California is urging Meta not to implement its forthcoming commercial terms on November 20, his office announced Tuesday.
  > 
  > In a letter sent to Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg, Schiff called on the company to respond by Monday, November 2 with the specific steps it will take to address user concerns.
  > 
  > “Forcing publishers to choose between surrendering their off-platform IP or forfeiting a primary channel of public communication is a gross abuse of Meta’s power as a powerful platform with critical access to consumers,” Schiff wrote. “Meta must not nullify federal IP protections and fair market competition through the unilateral insertion of fine print into terms of service contracts.”
  > 
  > Specifically, Schiff told Meta that it should “provide explicit guarantees that external technical boundaries like paywalls and robots.txt protocols will be fully respected.”
  > 
  > “Meta’s verbal assurances that it will not supersede robots.txt files, which are voluntary server flags, do not appear to constitute enforceable legal protections,” Schiff wrote. “Without the insertion of specific language codifying Meta’s policy to respect robots.txt signals into the latest contractual terms, users lack any reliable means by which to protect their content.”
  > 
  > Schiff’s office did not immediately respond to a request for comment.
  > 
  > Schiff is the latest elected official in the United States to raise concerns about Meta’s commercial terms, which have now drawn bipartisan criticism. On Friday, Republican Senator Marsha Blackburn of Tennessee said that the terms “indicate Meta is attempting to exploit American creators and businesses by granting itself sweeping authority to scrape content from any website associated with Facebook and Instagram business accounts.”
  > 
  > Blackburn, who chairs the Senate Judiciary Subcommittee on Privacy, Technology and the Law, called on the company to withdraw the terms.
  > 
  > The terms were previously set to take effect on October 30. Because the terms offered no way to opt out, news of them set off an outcry from news publishers, politicians, and press associations on both sides of the Atlantic. Meta then delayed the effective date of its new terms of service for business accounts to November 20.
  > 
  > On Tuesday, a coalition of Norwegian news publishers sued Meta over the terms and is asking the Oslo District Court to block the terms from going into effect. The legal action, led by the Norwegian Media Companies Association, is the first known lawsuit filed against Meta over its commercial terms.
  > 
  > Meta’s controversial terms
  > October 15Meta’s new commercial terms will allow the scraping of news sites, legal experts say
  > October 17Legal experts warn Meta’s new terms still grant broad scraping permissions, despite language changes
  > October 21U.K. politicians and German publishers call on regulators to pause Meta’s new terms before October 30 deadline
  > October 22Publishers pressure Meta to nix new terms that would let the company scrape their websites
  > October 26Meta delays rollout of terms that would allow broad scraping of news sites. Legal experts are still skeptical.
  > October 27 ★“A gross abuse of Meta’s power”: California senator Adam Schiff tells Meta to scrap its new terms of service
  > October 28Meta pledges to consult publishers before finalizing its new terms
  > 
  > Nieman Lab first reported on the terms on October 15. They would allow the mass scraping of websites “associated with” business accounts on Facebook, Instagram, WhatsApp, and Threads. Virtually every news organization has at least one business account. On October 16, Meta removed the word “train” from its terms and limited the use of future scraped content to “advertising and business products.”
  > 
  > “We are extending the effective date of our updated Commercial Terms and Self-Serve Ad Terms to November 20 to allow more time for us to explain to our partners and stakeholders what’s changing and what’s not — including how Meta uses web crawlers and the fact that content owners will still be able to use robots.txt to signal preferences,” a Meta spokesperson told Nieman Lab in a statement Friday.
  > 
  > A Meta spokesperson said on October 17 that the company revised the language because it “wasn’t clear in the initial consolidation of terms that robots.txt preferences are respected. News sites, like all other content owners, still have the ability to disable the crawler via robots.txt flags. That has been the case, and it has not changed.”
  > 
  > As of October 27, Meta has made no other changes to the terms aside from the implementation date. No version of the terms includes language about respecting robots.txt preferences.
  > 
  > Read the full letter here.

### 11. The Independent Journalism Atlas looks at “wells” and “springs” in news deserts
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：[原文链接](<https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/the-independent-journalism-atlas-looks-at-wells-and-springs-in-news-deserts/>)
- 发布时间：Mon, 26 Oct 2026 19:56:55 +0000
- 全文：
  > What do we mean when we talk about news deserts?
  > 
  > The term has become a shorthand for areas devoid of local news coverage. But a new essay from the Independent Journalism Atlas recalls that when Penny Abernathy popularized the term through her research, she used it to describe a more nuanced picture:
  > 
  > To show what information can look like in news deserts, the Independent Journalism Atlas — an initiative that aims to “[document] independent creators who inform, educate, and advance public knowledge outside traditional media structures,” cofounded by Liz Kelly Nelson, Justin Bank, and 2025 Nieman Fellow Ryan Kellett — took a closer look at 46 of the 238 news desert counties identified in this year’s State of Local News report from Medill.
  > 
  > “Look at the ground and news moves like water,” writes Bank. “It doesn’t stop at a county line. It runs off the surface, sinks underground and comes up wherever someone digs.” The team found that “in more places than the desert map shows, someone has picked up a shovel.” (That might help explain why people who live in news deserts don’t feel deprived of local news.) The team identifies a few patterns in how information looks and travels in news deserts, including “surface” information, wells, and springs.
  > 
  > Posts from the local sheriff, weather alerts, and obituaries from the funeral home are all examples of “surface” information that “runs off,” Bank writes, typically telling you what happened without telling you why.
  > 
  > Wells are newsrooms that form when a local paper shutters. The team points to the Anaconda Weekly Review in Deer Lodge County, Montana, as an example of a well appearing in a county Medill identified as a news desert.
  > 
  > Springs, meanwhile, are “harder to spot” than wells because they’re smaller and more ad hoc. The Independent Journalism Atlas applies this label to efforts like the Gallatin County Board Illinois Unofficial Facebook page, which livestreams the board’s public meetings and posts its agendas.
  > 
  > The team also paid attention to coverage of news deserts by outlets in neighboring counties. They found high school sports are often a gateway to coverage, pointing to a channel covering southern Illinois high school sports that interviewed the principal in Harrisburg, the seat of Saline County, another news desert county.
  > 
  > In recent years, efforts to “map” local news through different lenses have proliferated. The Independent Journalism Atlas is one such effort, focused on news creators. A better understanding of information sources in news deserts matters, Bank argues, because “you can’t tend a basin you haven’t mapped.”
  > 
  > Read the full essay here.

### 12. I went looking for the source of one little statistic. That was easier said than done.
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：[原文链接](<https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/i-went-looking-for-the-source-of-one-little-statistic-that-was-easier-said-than-done/>)
- 发布时间：Mon, 26 Oct 2026 15:17:15 +0000
- 全文：
  > Apparently, 75% of your emotions are generated by smell.
  > 
  > Or that’s what the internet says, anyway. The statistic appears everywhere from marketing books and articles to academic papers about the power of scent. As a perfumer (someone who creates fragrances) and journalist who writes about these things, I came across it several times, and when I finally wanted to cite it for an article about the impact of scent on mood and memory, I went looking for the original research.
  > 
  > But I couldn’t find it.
  > 
  > This wasn’t a totally new experience for me. One time, while working on a workplace story as the editor of a hospitality trade magazine, I couldn’t find the source of an employee retention statistic, so I wrote the people who included it in a study. They wrote back to say they had no idea what I was talking about.
  > 
  > In this case, there was no such dead end. I followed link after link, figuring surely I would get to the bottom of it, but every source pointed to another source, which pointed to another, and somehow none of them led to the original research I was looking for.
  > 
  > I asked ChatGPT, Google Gemini and Claude for help. They gave me links to wellness blogs, aromatherapy companies, and authoritative-looking PDF reports. But as I ruled each one out as the original source, the answers became more circular, and the trail ate itself.
  > 
  > Me: Where did the statistic that 75% of emotions are generated by smell come from?
  > 
  > Google Gemini The Sense of Smell Institute.
  > 
  > Me: Great. Which study?
  > 
  > Gemini: There isn’t a specific study. The statistic was popularized through scent marketing research.
  > 
  > Me: So where did the number come from?
  > 
  > Gemini: Martin Lindstrom’s 2005 book Brand Sense.
  > 
  > Me: Does the book contain the original research?
  > 
  > Gemini: Lindstrom commissioned extensive research into sensory branding through the research firm Millward Brown.
  > 
  > Me: Does that research establish that 75 percent of emotions are generated by smell?
  > 
  > Gemini: Would you like to know how lavender and citrus can influence your mood?
  > 
  > A breakthrough! Or so I thought
  > Lindstrom did look like a real lead. Several writers and businesses traced the number to the Danish author’s 2005 book Brand Sense: Sensory Secrets Behind the Stuff We Buy. Simon & Schuster’s page for the book says it outright: “Research shows that a full 75% of our emotions are in fact generated by what we smell.” Aha!
  > 
  > I bought the book and read it. While there is talk of emotions and the senses, the number isn’t in there.
  > 
  > Next, I wrote to Lindstrom’s company asking them to point me toward the original research. They sent me a link to a copy of Brand Sense and told me I would find the research in the book.
  > 
  > I tried another route. Brand Sense was based in large part on an international study by market research company Millward Brown. The 75% figure has been attributed directly to that research. So I went looking for the Millward Brown study. But I couldn’t find it anywhere.
  > 
  > Does 75% even make sense?
  > There were other questions: Does the stat even make scientific sense? How would you measure the percentage of emotions generated by smell? Do people with no sense of smell experience 75% fewer emotions? And what about emotions that seem to have nothing to do with the senses at all, like grief over someone’s death, a breakup, or anxiety over losing your job?
  > 
  > I asked Kristen Lindquist, a social and affective neuroscientist and psychology professor at Ohio State University who studies emotion. She had never heard of the statistic.
  > 
  > “I imagine if somebody had done that study I would be aware of it,” she told me.
  > 
  > There is a relationship between smell and emotion, and it may be possible to measure something like this, she said. Researchers could, for example, ask people throughout the day how they’re feeling and whether what they’re feeling is related to something they’re seeing, hearing, touching or smelling. But that still wouldn’t make emotion the product of one sense. “Emotions really arise from all of our sensations,” Lindquist said, including signals from within our bodies. Our brains process all of that information together to figure out what is happening and what it means for us.
  > 
  > So, what about that breakup?
  > 
  > “If you get dumped and you smell some nice cookies, are you going to feel 75% better?” Lindquist said. “Probably not.”
  > 
  > When repetition becomes evidence
  > There’s a name for the particular way an unsupported claim can acquire the appearance of fact through citation: citogenesis. Coined by Randall Munroe in an XKCD comic, the term describes the process by which an unsupported claim gets repeatedly cited until the citations themselves make it look like truth.
  > 
  > I called Jodi Schneider, an associate professor at the University of Wisconsin-Madison Information School whose research includes scientific evidence and citation integrity, to talk about it. I sent her the 75% statistic, and she went looking for the source herself.
  > 
  > “It was not a casual ‘Let me look for 20 minutes, and there it is,'” she told me. “I would find a thing, [it] would go to something else, and that would be a dead end.”
  > 
  > Her conclusion: “It’s hard to source.”
  > 
  > The problem, Schneider said, is that repeating a claim is much easier than investigating it. “Somebody says something, it sounds like a thing you’d like to know or repeat, and you don’t trace it back to where it came from. You just pass it on.” Then repetition becomes the “evidence,” and “if you hear the same thing from multiple places, it’s harder to question.”
  > 
  > This psychological phenomenon is known as the illusory truth effect: We’re more likely to believe information we’ve encountered before. The effect was first demonstrated in the 1970s and has since been replicated many times. More unsettlingly, research has found that repetition can make a false claim seem more believable even when it contradicts something we know to be true.
  > 
  > In other words, seeing the same statistic everywhere doesn’t just make it look well sourced; it makes it feel true.
  > 
  > It’s hard to fully check every claim. Writers build arguments from dozens of pieces of information, and tracing each one all the way back to its origin can take an absurd amount of time.
  > 
  > Newsrooms have spent years cutting staff and budgets, including the fact-checkers and copy editors whose job was to catch this kind of thing. At the same time, the volume of information journalists have to sort through has exploded. Finding a source for a claim can take seconds. Establishing where it actually came from can take hours (or an eternity).
  > 
  > Meanwhile, the consequences of not checking facts compound. Once a bad claim gets published, it becomes another source for the next journalist, another search result for the next reader and another apparent confirmation that the claim must be true.
  > 
  > Then came AI
  > AI is making that problem worse. It repeats the same unsupported claims while stripping away some of the clues that might otherwise make us suspicious of the source.
  > 
  > “The polish that generative AI can put on just about anything is already a challenge,” Schneider said. More importantly, “Generative AI is really, really good at being persuasive, even if the claims inside the persuasion have no basis in reality.”
  > 
  > Most people are not going to track a statistic through six articles, buy the book everyone cites, email the author, and start calling academics. They don’t have the time or energy for that, and they shouldn’t have to do it anyway. One of journalism’s roles is to provide information that’s more reliable than TikTok or whatever nonsense Google Gemini spits out.
  > 
  > When we get it wrong, we don’t just add another bad source to the pile. We give the claim credibility and put it in front of more people. And once they’ve seen it, the illusory truth effect suggests they’re more likely to believe it the next time. (Research has also found that repeated misinformation is more likely to be repeated than new misinformation, and can continue to influence people even after it has been corrected.)
  > 
  > Nobody is going to die because they believe smell generates 75 percent of their emotions. But replace that claim with one about a vaccine, a war, an election or a medical treatment and the same failure becomes considerably less benign.
  > 
  > That’s why the difference between finding a source and finding the source matters. I found hundreds of sources for the 75% statistic. I still haven’t found the source. That doesn’t mean it’s not out there.
  > 
  > I just can’t find it.
  > 
  > Can you find the source of the 75% stat? Email staff@niemanlab.org.
  > 
  > Elizabeth Bromstein is a writer, editor, and perfumer.

### 13. Meta delays rollout of terms that would allow broad scraping of news sites. Legal experts are still skeptical.
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：[原文链接](<https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/meta-delays-rollout-of-terms-that-would-allow-broad-scraping-of-news-sites-legal-experts-are-still-skeptical/>)
- 发布时间：Mon, 26 Oct 2026 09:00:31 +0000
- 全文：
  > Meta has delayed the effective date of its new terms of service for business accounts to November 20 in the face of mounting pressure from the global news industry.
  > 
  > Nieman Lab first reported on the terms on October 15. They would allow for the mass scraping of websites “associated with” business accounts on Facebook, Instagram, WhatsApp, and Threads. Virtually every news organization has at least one business account. On October 16, Meta removed the word “train” from its terms and limited the use of future scraped content to “advertising and business products.”
  > 
  > The terms were previously set to take effect on October 30, with no way to opt out, setting off an outcry from news publishers, politicians, and press associations on both sides of the Atlantic.
  > 
  > “We are extending the effective date of our updated Commercial Terms and Self-Serve Ad Terms to November 20 to allow more time for us to explain to our partners and stakeholders what’s changing and what’s not — including how Meta uses web crawlers and the fact that content owners will still be able to use robots.txt to signal preferences,” a Meta spokesperson told Nieman Lab in a statement Friday.
  > 
  > A Meta spokesperson said on October 17 that the company revised the language because it “wasn’t clear in the initial consolidation of terms that robots.txt preferences are respected. News sites, like all other content owners, still have the ability to disable the crawler via robots.txt flags. That has been the case, and it has not changed.”
  > 
  > As of October 23, Meta has made no other changes to the terms aside from the implementation date. No versions of the terms include language about respecting robots.txt preferences.
  > 
  > “What needs to happen next is for the Competition and Markets Authority, and other regulators around the world, to secure clear, public commitments from Meta to abandon the offending elements of the new Terms before they take effect,” Bamber said.
  > 
  > Are you considering changing how you use Meta-owned platforms? Let us know: staff@niemanlab.org.

### 14. Publishers pressure Meta to nix new terms that would let the company scrape their websites
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：[原文链接](<https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/publishers-pressure-meta-to-nix-new-terms-that-would-let-it-scrape-their-websites/>)
- 发布时间：Thu, 22 Oct 2026 19:26:54 +0000
- 全文：
  > The largest news publishing trade association in the United States is calling on Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg to suspend the implementation of the company’s new commercial terms of service, set to go into effect on October 30.
  > 
  > In an open letter to Zuckerberg published Thursday, News/Media Alliance’s president and CEO Danielle Coffey wrote that Meta should “withdraw these terms and should hold discussions around future changes with NMA and publishers.”
  > 
  > The terms, first reported by Nieman Lab on October 15, would allow for the mass scraping of websites “associated with” business accounts on Facebook, Instagram, WhatsApp, and Threads. There is currently no option to opt out. On October 16, Meta removed the word “train” from its terms and limited the use of future scraped content to “advertising and business products.” But legal experts told Nieman Lab the revised terms would still permit mass scraping.
  > 
  > “News publishers will be forced into providing their valuable journalism to support the development of products that may be used to compete with them for readers and advertisers, under duress and without compensation,” Coffey said in her letter. “It is wholly unreasonable for the mere use of a Meta business account to require publishers to grant unrestricted access to their websites (and even content posted by their users), for the benefit of Meta to develop its business products.”
  > 
  > NMA represents more than 2,000 news publishers in the United States. The discussions, Coffey said, should focus on four core issues:
  > 
  > The Association of Information Media (AMI) in Spain, which represents more than 100 news outlets in the country, echoed its support of the letter in a statement on Thursday.
  > 
  > “Technological innovation and the development of artificial intelligence cannot come at the expense of content creators’ rights or the economic viability of professional journalism,” the AMI statement reads in part. “Defending publishers’ rights does not mean hindering innovation, but rather ensuring that it develops while respecting the law, competition, and media pluralism.”
  > 
  > Read the full News/Media Alliance letter here.

### 15. The Ringer bets on Substack’s network over its own website
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：[原文链接](<https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/the-ringer-bets-on-substacks-network-over-its-own-website/>)
- 发布时间：Thu, 22 Oct 2026 19:13:23 +0000
- 全文：
  > “Very interesting that The Ringer’s writers write on Substack and not…The Ringer.” “What’s the point of the website then?” “Hold up. You’re telling me The Ringer would rather make money on writing than not make money on writing?”
  > 
  > These were some of the responses after The Ringer’s Zach Lowe announced his new newsletter. The Lowe Post, which covers the NBA, joins The Ringer’s Sean Fennessey, Tom Pelissero, and Bryan Curtis on Substack. (The Lowe Post is $10 a month, and has already become one of Substack’s bestselling sports newsletters. Fennessey’s newsletter is $5 a month; Pelissero’s is $8 a month, and Curtis’s is free.)
  > 
  > Though podcasting has been his main gig for a while now, Zach Lowe is one of the best sports writers in the country — witty, warm-hearted, and a true lover of basketball and the Mets. In his inaugural newsletter, he wrote about how he feels his sports analysis loses a step when he’s only talking, not writing.
  > 
  > On the one hand, of course Lowe should have a newsletter. But his Substack, and those of his colleagues, are not independent side hustles. Each has Spotify and The Ringer branding.
  > 
  > As a digital media business, The Ringer has always been flexible and opportunistic. It was initially published on Medium when it launched in 2016. The publication moved to an ad and tech partnership with Vox in 2017. It was acquired by Spotify for $250 million in 2020 and, five years later, signed a distribution deal with Netflix to bring some of its video podcasts (including The Bill Simmons Show, The Zach Lowe Show, and The Rewatchables) to the platform. The website has always been free to read.
  > 
  > The Ringer declined to comment on its Substack strategy and would not tell me whether the writers receive a share of subscription revenue. (The lack of comment has garnered its own press coverage.) Though The Ringer has referred to the new newsletters as being in “partnership” with Substack, there’s no separate financial relationship between the two companies, Substack confirmed.
  > 
  > If you find this a little strange, you’re not wrong. The Substack-Ringer whatever-this-is is a confusing move that is raising a lot of very 2026 questions. Here are some things I’m wondering.
  > 
  > Why is The Ringer giving up on its own website?
  > While Grantland — the beloved sports writing site created by Ringer founder Bill Simmons in 2011 and shuttered by ESPN in 2015 — was known for its long-form journalism, The Ringer is all-in on podcasts. Simmons jokes on nearly every episode of his own show that his “fingers stopped working” years ago and that his days writing columns are behind him.
  > 
  > Today, most of the links on The Ringer’s homepage will bring you somewhere else. The homepage’s function is closer to a landing page or linktree than a news website. Many links redirect to Spotify, where listeners can watch or listen to Ringer podcasts. Now, with the newsletter launches, several links whisk you off The Ringer and over to Substack.
  > 
  > Burned by Facebook and forever-shifting algorithmic winds, many publishers have focused on building on their own channels in recent years. The theory is that direct-to-reader news products like newsletters, apps, and events will help publishers control audience connections and insulate them from inevitable platform pivots.
  > 
  > But though Substack was once synonymous with independent writers, its perks are luring more and more established publications onto the platform. Some, like The Ringer, are sharing Substack-exclusive content. Others, like New York magazine and The Wall Street Journal, are redistributing work that exists elsewhere on the internet.
  > 
  > One major pull? Substack has morphed into a full-on social media platform, albeit one where publishers can directly monetize and capture email addresses for themselves.
  > 
  > In the past six months, the app has generated nearly 800,000 paid subscriptions, according to Substack, and more than half of those subscriptions came directly from its Twitter-like Notes feed. Network effects account for more than half of all subscriptions on the platform and about 30% of paid subscriptions, a spokesperson said.
  > 
  > Substack offers publishers another major perk: little friction when signing up or converting to a paid subscription. I subscribed to The Lowe Post with a single click. I didn’t have to fill out my credit card info or even my email address — since I’m a paying subscriber to other Substacks, it was a seamless transaction.
  > 
  > Media journalist Simon Owens has been tracking the rise of traditional media outlets on Substack (on Substack, naturally).
  > 
  > “I’d compare The Ringer’s strategy to publishers producing native content for YouTube,” Owens said. “The company recognizes that Substack has its own audience ecosystem, and it may be easier to convert users into subscribers directly on the platform than to convince them to visit The Ringer’s website.”
  > 
  > “What’s more, these payments are facilitated through The Ringer’s own Stripe account, so in the worst case scenario it could easily move its subscribers over to its website if it decides to abandon Substack,” he added. “So I think there’s little downside.”
  > 
  > Is this a bad omen for the open web?
  > “Open web” is an ill-defined concept. Sometimes people use it to mean the public portion of the internet that’s indexable by search engines. Others mean, basically, websites — anything you can reach on a web browser at a URL. Whatever the semantics, people have been shoveling dirt on “the open web” for years. (This New York Times obituary noting the rise of paywalls, apps, and tiers of access was published back in 2010.) But the death notices have picked up with the rise of AI.
  > 
  > Google Search referrals to publisher websites are down 40.2% year over year, according to Chartbeat. And AI chatbots are not sending users to news sites. Referrals from ChatGPT, Perplexity, Claude, and the like were just 0.01% of traffic to publishers and declining, Chartbeat found.
  > 
  > Even before it was acquired by Spotify, The Ringer’s primary business was podcasts, so it’s never been as reliant on search as text-first publishers. The Ringer can afford to deprioritize its website more than most. But all kinds of publishers are having to respond to a real shift in the way people consume news and content.
  > 
  > What does this mean for other publishers?
  > Kendall Baker, senior writer and head of newsletters for Yahoo Sports, knows a thing or two about email newsletters. He says The Ringer’s move exemplifies a news consumption trend that’s only picking up speed.
  > 
  > “Most people’s general way of finding information or consuming information online is that they’re opening their phone and going straight to a social media app and starting to scroll,” Baker said.
  > 
  > Another app that most U.S. adults open up every day? Their email.
  > 
  > “If I want an engaged audience on a daily basis, relying on them coming to find me versus going to them would be silly — and it’s becoming more and more silly,” he said. “I think that’s partially what’s driving this. Newsletters are an obvious solution there.”
  > 
  > The Ringer’s move is also a bet on the strength of individual personalities. Substack doesn’t (yet) offer bundles, so a reader who wants all of The Ringer’s paid Substack content will have to fork over something like $23/month — more than a subscription to New York magazine or Apple News and a little less than a full New York Times bundle. Even with the strong parasocial relationships The Ringer enjoys — enough to fuel many, many subreddits — the pricing was a common complaint online.
  > 
  > Many publishers will not want to cannibalize pricier subscriptions by selling access to a single popular writer. Offering a whole-site subscription also lets readers discover (and hopefully fall in love with!) more of your content.
  > 
  > “It seems in the Substack era, you’re writing for people who really like you,” Simmons said on a recent podcast about sportswriting. “And you’re getting that audience but then, how do you pull other people in? That’s going to be the big question over the next five to 10 years.”
  > 
  > “Obviously we have a lot of writing on The Ringer dot com that we’re very proud of,” Simmons added, pointing to a NFL Take-O-Meter piece by The Ringer’s Austin Gayle. “But will it ever be like 2011 again? I don’t know.”
  > 
  > “You mean, when we launched Grantland?” The Ringer’s Bryan Curtis asked.
  > 
  > “No, just that era. How many writers were coming up — the energy behind it, and all the different styles,” Simmons said. “I wonder if we’ll ever have that many writers again trying that many different things.”

### 16. In the AI age, publishers who understand what readers want will do best, a new report argues
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：[原文链接](<https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/in-the-ai-age-publishers-who-understand-what-readers-want-will-do-best-a-new-report-argues/>)
- 发布时间：Thu, 22 Oct 2026 15:23:35 +0000
- 全文：
  > AI inverts the traditional journalism hierarchy: Instead of reporters gathering information, editors deciding how to present it, and newsrooms releasing the same final story to everyone, AI agents deliver what readers ask for, refining or expanding on information as requested.
  > 
  > It’s “an interactive process in a way the current news system isn’t,” write the authors of “The Three-Layer Cake: Public-Interest Information in an AI-Intermediated Age,” released Wednesday by the Tow-Knight Center for Journalism Futures at the Craig Newmark Graduate School of Journalism at CUNY. (The cake’s three layers are “the products that identify what people want to know, the systems that synthesize answers, and the organizations that produce the underlying reporting.”)
  > 
  > Some interesting ideas from the report by Gina Chua and Adiel Kaplan:
  > 
  > This new age may be better for some readers. The authors write:
  > 
  > There are people the old system never reached, or never reached well. A parent who hears of school budget cuts may want to know if their child’s elementary school art class will survive. No newsroom would assign that story. It matters to a handful of families whose children attend a single school, and the exact answer is uncertain. A well-designed AI system could pull together what is known from reporting and context and offer a nuanced response — fulfilling an information need that previously did not have enough economic power to be met directly.
  > 
  > Similarly, minority communities whose concerns didn’t rise to the level of interest of a city newspaper might have rarely seen their perspectives reflected in coverage in the past. Coverage in the mainstream media of the AIDS epidemic in the early 1980s was often focused on the risk to the heterosexual population rather than on the gay community it ravaged. An AI system that can access information from and about underserved communities could rectify that.
  > 
  > Publishers opting out could make the information environment worse. If news organizations seek to prevent AI from using their content, they may contribute to a poorer “information commons,” the authors argue. It “works as a strategy for individual organizations, but cedes the broader mission of providing information to the vast majority of the public to others who may not have the public’s interests at heart.”
  > 
  > Scoops and exclusives are a “luxury” — and they’re losing value fast.
  > 
  > Even a pivot to investigative reporting, as important a public good as that is, is not necessarily a solution. Much of investigative journalism is summarizable once it is published, even if the original story breaks important news and is a delight to read. As Francesco Marconi noted, a multi-month investigation could cost a newsroom $200,000 in resources, then drop in economic value to close to zero within minutes of publication. Both investigations and scoops can have huge social value and help build a brand, but economically, they provide little lasting value to their newsrooms — and even less so in an AI-intermediated landscape.
  > 
  > “Commoditized news” isn’t saving anyone. Publishers have to make “hard choices” about what they’ll cover, the authors write:
  > 
  > In an AI-intermediated world, commoditized news carries little value, especially if it’s already available in some other form. That’s not to say that day-to-day coverage of municipal government, a core task of many local newsrooms, should go away; only that its coverage needs to be differentiated from what could be found elsewhere or summarized from a meeting transcript, for it to hold value. Differentiation might involve adding context to the coverage through original reporting, or asking questions that wouldn’t otherwise be asked at a press conference.
  > 
  > The deeper and more specialized a newsroom’s area of coverage and expertise is, the less likely it is to be duplicated by other organizations — and that provides both pricing power and closer links to the communities that value such information.
  > 
  > The report is here. Read excerpts from a talk that coauthor Chua gave to Nieman Fellows here.

### 17. U.K. politicians and German publishers call on regulators to pause Meta’s new terms before October 30 deadline
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：[原文链接](<https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/u-k-politicians-and-german-publishers-call-on-regulators-to-pause-metas-new-terms-before-october-30-deadline/>)
- 发布时间：Wed, 21 Oct 2026 20:37:07 +0000
- 全文：
  > The U.K. competition and consumer protection authority sent a formal letter to Meta to discuss its new commercial terms of service.
  > 
  > On Tuesday, Will Hayter, the executive director for digital markets at the Competition and Markets Authority (CMA), shared the update during a session of the communications and digital committee of British Parliament, after a member of the House of Lords pressed him about Meta’s new terms. Hayter said he scheduled a meeting with the tech giant today to seek clarity on the new terms, though the CMA declined to provide any further details.
  > 
  > Last week, Nieman Lab broke the news that the terms would permit mass scraping of websites “associated with” business accounts on Facebook, Instagram, WhatsApp, and Threads. The new terms are slated to take effect on October 30, and there is currently no option to opt out. Virtually every news organization owns at least one Meta business account.
  > 
  > “Are you going to pause the deadline? I mean, [it’s in] a week,” Veronica Wadley, Baroness Fleet and a former editor of the Evening Standard, said during the session of Parliament. “You know this includes almost every U.K. news publisher and it is clearly uncompetitive leverage of Meta’s strategic market position.”
  > 
  > In the same session, Sarah Cardell, the CEO of CMA, said the authority had limited intervention options because it has not formally designated Meta as an “entrenched market power” (something it has done in the past for Google and Apple). Cardell said other competition enforcement actions are a possibility, but the CMA is “urgently” seeking more information from Meta before making any judgment.
  > 
  > “Meta intends to automatically capture, copy, and store the complete websites and archives of publishers and analyze them for its own automated and non-automated commercial products,” reads a joint statement from the associations. “This makes access to Meta’s platforms contingent on the free surrender of the entire digital publishing product. This is blatant theft.”
  > 
  > The statement alleges that Meta’s new terms could be in violation of the E.U. Digital Markets Act, which was passed in 2022 to curb the dominance of big tech companies and create fairer competition across the region. The publishers cited a specific clause that guarantees “fair, reasonable, and non-discriminatory general conditions of access for business users.”
  > 
  > The uproar from European publishers echoes similar opposition to the new terms from major U.S. publisher associations. Jason Kint, the CEO of Digital Content Next, has been vocal about his skepticism of the new terms since Nieman Lab’s story broke last week.
  > 
  > “Meta’s supposed Friday night correction to its new commercial terms is a farce,” he wrote in a statement published on Tuesday. “Exactly what you’d expect from a gatekeeper notorious for abusing its market power.”

### 18. The Wall Street Journal’s new AI tool, “Doppler,” forecasts the issues that will shape the midterm elections
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：[原文链接](<https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/the-wall-street-journals-new-ai-tool-doppler-forecasts-the-issues-that-will-shape-the-midterm-elections/>)
- 发布时间：Wed, 21 Oct 2026 19:05:45 +0000
- 全文：
  > More than 100 million voters are expected to cast ballots in the 2026 U.S. midterm elections.
  > 
  > In recent election cycles, political reporting based on voter polling has drawn criticism, prompted scrutiny, and found defenders. As the debate over the value of polling continues, The Wall Street Journal is exploring new ways to cover public opinion and break out of traditional horse race political coverage.
  > 
  > In late September, the Journal published its first story using Doppler News AI, a custom tool built to help expand the publication’s midterms coverage. So far, Doppler has ingested more than 25 million TikTok videos, roughly 500,000 podcast episodes, and thousands of Instagram videos and cable news segments. The Journal uses the tool to monitor trends across the internet and to identify voter priorities, concerns, and sentiments that traditional polling might miss.
  > 
  > “We’re not trying to predict the election, but people encounter politics through a lot of different lanes: culture, work, money, family, community. We wanted to tap into that and listen in on those conversations,” said Aja Whitaker-Moore, the deputy editor-in-chief of the Journal. “We’re taking a massive set of information…and telling you, from a statistical standpoint: ‘Here’s what people are talking about. Here are the questions they’re asking. Here are the places where those conversations are happening.'”
  > 
  > Other newsrooms have leaned on AI “visibility tools” in the past to support their reporting. That includes using large language models (LLMs) to transcribe and summarize “manosphere” podcasts, school board meetings, and local talk radio shows. But Doppler goes further.
  > 
  > Rather than using AI-generated summaries to generate leads or help beat reporters monitor online content more efficiently, the Journal uses Doppler to conduct large-scale AI analyses quickly — and publishes those insights for readers.
  > 
  > So far, analyses produced by Doppler have led to four major midterms stories in the Journal, with more expected to run before Election Day. For now, each story requires a custom treatment, with engineers and data journalists working alongside the D.C. politics team to refine the methodology, toggle on and off different features, and audit the results.
  > 
  > “The tool is only as good as the questions that you ask it,” said Whitaker-Moore. The Journal holds a weekly editorial meeting leading up to Election Day to workshop story angles and find new threads to explore.
  > 
  > @wallstreetjournal
  > 
  > A new in-house AI tool lets WSJ reporters analyze trends in online conversation to help understand the political mood of the country ahead of the midterm elections. A previous post paired a visual of TikToks from across the internet with a headline that identified them as conservative podcasters.
  > 
  > ♬ original sound – The Wall Street Journal
  > 
  > A story published on October 8 analyzed over 30,000 hours of podcasts and documented how, in recent weeks, right-leaning podcasters had started discussing “affordability” as often as left-leaning podcasters, despite President Trump calling the affordability crisis a “hoax.”
  > 
  > Another story published on October 1 traced anti-AI sentiment, including opposition to Flock surveillance cameras, by analyzing more than four million TikTok videos and thousands of hours of podcasts. The Journal found that posters who shared anti-Flock sentiments disagreed on other political positions about 12% of the time, three times the average rate across other political topics tracked — putting the issue “at the top of the list of positions that unite creators who are otherwise at odds.”
  > 
  > For a story published on October 15, the Journal pulled content about 67 Democratic politicians from its Doppler database. The analysis showed that the Democratic politician currently receiving the most favorable commentary is Angie Nixon, the progressive Senate nominee in Florida. About 70% of TikTok videos posted about her in recent weeks were favorable. She was followed by Georgia Senator Jon Ossoff at 54%. Meanwhile, Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer ranked the lowest in the analysis; just 3% of videos posted about him were favorable.
  > 
  > [img wide]Courtesy of The Wall Street Journal[/img]
  > 
  > Whitaker-Moore said the idea for Doppler was inspired by conversations with Washington bureau chief Damian Paletta and politics editor Christopher Matthews. They thought the coverage of the 2024 U.S. election missed important undercurrents in public opinion that never surfaced in polling. And though reporters acknowledged the power of podcasts in that election cycle, there weren’t many standardized ways to capture what was being said across those channels.
  > 
  > Coming out of those conversations, Whitaker-Moore posed a question to Rob Barry, who leads the Journal’s data journalism team: “Can you build something that can eat the internet and then feed it back to us?”
  > 
  > The Journal had already built its own podcast transcription and summarization tool, Orca. (The name refers to the tight-knit pods in which orcas travel.) Orca had collected countless hours of audio and YouTube videos, not just of political podcasts but from genres like health and wellness.
  > 
  > The Journal’s experience with TikTok-based investigations made the social media platform’s videos another natural source of information for Doppler. It’s nearly impossible to create a comprehensive database of TikTok videos, or even a truly random sample. Instead, Barry’s team used a monitoring tool that relies on TikTok’s own recommendation algorithms to identify the accounts it should track.
  > 
  > “Every day we’re adding tons of data across all the different platforms that we’re looking at,” Barry told me. “The database is growing in the background constantly.”
  > 
  > To make sense of that data, Doppler has a tech stack that includes different types of AI models, including LLMs. The model used depends on the specific reporting question.
  > 
  > Embedding models have been central to some of stories published so far. These AI models convert text or images into vectors — lists of numbers that capture the meaning of each piece of content. By vectorizing the content, Doppler can map out the ideas in a dataset and see where they naturally cluster together.
  > 
  > The Journal’s data journalism team has used vectorization in the past, including in its Pulitzer Prize–winning story that mapped out Elon Musk’s entire history of tweets. In Doppler, vectorization facilitates a kind of internet radar, helping reporters identify where patterns are forming across its social media and podcasting database.
  > 
  > For now, the Journal’s political reporters can’t just start using Doppler on their own. The exact methodology has been tailored to each story published, with the help of engineers.
  > 
  > “There’s a lot of human judgment in this. My team would not have aimed for a tool that seeks to just produce stuff that we then put directly on the internet,” Barry said. “You still need that human intermediary, that human to apply the judgment and to ask the question.”
  > 
  > The Journal is attaching a short methodology to every story assisted by Doppler. It makes clear that AI was used to produce the piece, briefly explains how the analysis came together, and notes some of the tool’s limitations.
  > 
  > Despite manual spot checks of data classifications and other safeguards to make sure the tool’s outputs are accurate, Barry said, “there’s almost always going to be some level of error and uncertainty and blur around the edges.” The disclosures are one way to “give readers a sense for what that level of issue is.”
  > 
  > Looking ahead, the Journal is considering bringing Doppler to other desks in the newsroom. The data being collected is not inherently political, and it could be used to help answer a host of other reporting questions, drawing on insights scattered across the internet.
  >
